{"stdout": "REVISION_CHECKLIST.md\nSKILL.md\n# Final revision checklist\n\nRun this **after the draft is finished**, as a separate pass, before the paper\nis handed on. It is not a writing guide — the rest of `SKILL.md` is that. It is\nthe list of defects that survive a first draft *because* the author wrote it:\neach one is invisible from the inside and obvious to the first outside reader.\n\n**How to run it.** Re-read the whole draft once as an editor who did not write\nit. Then take the items below one at a time, against the full text — not from\nmemory of what you intended. For each item, either **fix the draft** or state in\none line why it already holds. A pass that produces no edits is a pass that was\nnot really run: assume at least a few of these apply to any first draft.\n\n---\n\n## 1. Plain, professional language\n\nWrite the plainest prose the field accepts. Formality is not complexity — a\ntop-venue paper reads *simply*; it is the ideas that are hard, not the\nsentences.\n\n- Test: could a competent researcher from a neighbouring subfield follow each\n  sentence on the first pass, at reading speed?\n- Fix: replace ornamental vocabulary with the ordinary word. Unpack stacked\n  noun phrases (\"gradient-based sample-efficiency degradation analysis\").\n  Split any sentence carrying more than one claim. Cut throat-clearing\n  (\"It is important to note that\", \"In this work, we importantly\").\n- Every term of art gets a one-clause definition at first use, including the\n  names this paper itself invents.\n- Test: list every noun phrase the paper uses for its own procedures,\n  conditions and metrics, and next to each write the term the field uses for\n  it. Any mismatch is lab slang a reader cannot search for (\"knob\" for a\n  decoding parameter, \"dose\" for intervention strength, \"arm\" for a condition,\n  \"twin\" for a matched control, \"hill-1\" for an effective number). Fix: rename\n  it to the field's term everywhere, captions and tables included; keep\n  established method names (attribution patching, DAS) as the field spells\n  them.\n\n## 2. The abstract is prose, not a results table\n\nAn abstract dense with numbers cannot be read — the reader has no axes,\nbaselines, or units in mind yet, so each number costs them more than it tells\nthem.\n\n- Test: count the numbers in the abstract. More than about three, and it is a\n  data dump. The headline measurement's own numbers (its effect and interval)\n  and its evidence grade are the abstract's point: they always stay, and a cut\n  never takes them.\n- Fix: keep only the headline results — the ones that would appear in a\n  one-sentence summary of the paper. Cut secondary and supporting numbers\n  first; move them to Results, where they sit next to the baseline and the\n  axis that make them mean something.\n- The abstract must state, in words: the problem, what was done, what was\n  found, and why it matters. A reader who stops after the abstract should be\n  able to say all four back.\n- A key null or negative result that bounds the headline (a held-out null, a\n  specification under which the effect vanishes) is stated in the abstract\n  too. Leaving it for Results overstates the finding to the reader who stops\n  here.\n\n## 3. One job per section\n\nSections leak in a first draft because the author writes what they know as they\nthink of it.\n\n- Test: read the Introduction alone. Does it contain method detail, result\n  tables, or a survey of prior work? Those belong to Method, Results, and\n  Related Work.\n- Test the reverse direction too, which is the half that gets missed: **no\n  later section may depend on a definition, formula, symbol, or piece of\n  notation that appears only in the Introduction.** If Method needs it, it is\n  defined in Method or in Preliminaries; the Introduction may motivate it, not\n  own it.\n- Fix: move the material to the section whose job it is, and leave a\n  forward-reference (\"we define this formally in Section 3\") if the\n  Introduction still needs to gesture at it.\n\n## 4. Conventional section names\n\nSection names are navigation, not titles. A reader scanning the contents must\nknow what is in each section *without reading it*.\n\n- Test: could this table of contents belong to any paper in the field? If a\n  heading names a concept the paper itself invented, it tells the reader\n  nothing until they have already read the section.\n- Fix: use the names the field uses — Introduction, Related Work,\n  Preliminaries, Method, Experiments, Results, Analysis, Discussion,\n  Limitations, Conclusion. Put the invented name in the section's first\n  sentence, or in a subsection heading underneath the conventional one.\n- Legitimate variants exist (\"Discussion and Related Work\" when related work\n  sits at the end). The bar is that the name says what kind of content follows.\n\n## 5. Related work, searched with the *final* vocabulary\n\nBy the end of the draft the work has a name, a metric, and a problem statement\nthat the project did not have when it started. The literature search that was\nrun at the beginning could not have used any of them.\n\n- Fix: run at least one more search now, using the draft's own final terms —\n  the contribution's name, the metric's name, the exact problem statement, and\n  the nearest baseline's name. Fetch real BibTeX (see `SKILL.md`) and cite what\n  comes back.\n- Also check the reference lists of the two or three closest papers already\n  cited; the nearest neighbour is very often cited by one of them.\n- An uncited close prior work is among the most common reasons a paper is\n  rejected, and it is entirely preventable at this point.\n\n## 6. Figure 1 carries the main idea\n\nThe first figure is the one every reader looks at, often before reading a word.\nIt must answer \"what is this work?\".\n\n- Test: shown only Figure 1 and its caption, could a reader say what the paper\n  proposes or studies?\n- Fix: Figure 1 is a concept overview of the method or central idea, drawn by\n  the image model — not one narrow comparison and not a secondary improvement,\n  however strong that result is. Its marker sits at the end of the\n  Introduction, and the headline data figure follows it there as Figure 2. If\n  the current first figure is a result, move it after the overview and promote\n  the overview to Figure 1. In the draft, specify one if there is none; the\n  LaTeX step cannot draw, so there it keeps the draft's overview first and\n  never demotes it.\n- The overview looks like the Figure 1s of the field's top recent papers named\n  in its \"Style references:\", and reads at print size: 16:9 (never wider than\n  2:1), 2 to 4 lettered panels read left to right, the paper's own objects on\n  one real worked example, about 80 to 120 words inside the image with the\n  headline number, labels of at most about 8 words, no slogan panel or generic\n  icon, and no display equation — that goes in the text and the caption.\n- Only a paper with no method or idea to draw opens with its data figure.\n- A correct figure in the wrong slot is still the wrong Figure 1.\n\n## 7. Report the whole study, not only the highlights\n\nIf the work covers N of something — metrics, models, datasets, configurations,\nseeds — then all N must be visible somewhere the reader can check them.\n\n- Test: state N explicitly, from the artifacts rather than from the draft. Now\n  find where all N appear. \"We evaluate 53 metrics\" followed by a figure\n  showing eight is a gap the reader will assume was chosen to flatter.\n- Fix: add the complete view — a full figure, or a complete table, in the body\n  or the Appendix. Highlighting a subset in the main text is good writing;\n  showing *only* that subset is not. The complete view usually belongs in the\n  Appendix, so the body stays within its budget (item 13).\n- The same applies to negative and null results from the study. They belong in\n  the paper.\n\n## 8. No implementation-internal references in the prose\n\nThe paper describes the work; the repository holds the implementation. A reader\ncannot follow a sentence that names a file they cannot see.\n\n- Test: search the draft, tables and captions included, for filenames, module\n  paths, line numbers, function names, class names, CLI flags, and variable\n  names from the codebase (`M0_density_end`, `CONTACT_REACH`, \"Exp. 5\",\n  \"k3circuit.py, line 482\"). The research report is held to the same rule.\n- Fix: state the rule, not the code that implements it. Not \"`eligibility.py`\n  declares E1 as ...\" but \"an item is eligible when ...\". A raw variable name\n  becomes a descriptive one (\"network density at period end\"), in the text\n  and in every table row and header. If the pointer is\n  genuinely useful, it goes in a footnote, an artifact link, or an appendix —\n  never in a sentence the reader has to parse.\n- Mathematical notation and algorithm names are not affected by this; they are\n  the paper's own vocabulary, not the implementation's.\n\n## 9. Consistency — several separate passes, one concern each\n\nInconsistency is the defect a first draft is *guaranteed* to have: the paper was\nwritten in pieces, over time, while the results were still moving. A single\n\"check it's consistent\" sweep finds almost nothing, because each concern needs a\ndifferent thing held in mind. Run these as **separate passes over the whole\ndocument**, one per entry below, and repeat any pass that produced an edit — a\nfix in one place routinely breaks agreement somewhere else. Each entry names the\npass, what to hold in mind while running it (in brackets), and the failure it\ncatches.\n\n- **Claim ↔ evidence** (every claim in the text) — a claim with no figure,\n  table, or number behind it; or one whose evidence shows something weaker\n  than claimed.\n- **Evidence ↔ claim** (every figure and table) — a result presented but never\n  discussed, and the reverse: something described in the text that is never\n  actually shown (see item 7).\n- **Numbers** (one value at a time, the artifact file open) — a number not\n  taken from the artifact output files, or the same quantity differing\n  between abstract, text, table, figure, and caption. Before writing that the\n  artifacts do not report something, search their output files for it.\n- **Comparisons** (each \"more than\", \"beats\", \"versus\") — two quantities\n  compared across different metrics or evaluations. A held-out Spearman gain\n  set beside a partial correlation is no comparison: compare on one metric\n  and one evaluation, or say the artifacts do not allow it.\n- **Figure ↔ caption ↔ table** (each figure) — the plotted quantity, the axis\n  label, the caption and any table showing the same result must carry the\n  same values under the same name; a panel coefficient labelled as a\n  standardised mean difference is two quantities. A figure with no data\n  behind it (a drawn network, a stylised map) says in its caption that it is\n  illustrative.\n- **Citations — placement** (each `[n]` in context) — a reference attached to a\n  claim it does not support, or supporting a claim it only mentions in\n  passing.\n- **Citations — integrity** (the bibliography) — cited but not listed; listed\n  but never cited; the same work under two entries; a fabricated or\n  unverified entry.\n- **Terminology** (one term at a time) — the same concept under two names, or\n  one name used for two concepts.\n- **Notation** (each symbol) — a symbol reused with a second meaning, or used\n  before it is defined (\"D = -0.42\" with no D defined). Define every symbol\n  where it first appears, tables and captions included.\n- **Cross-references** (each \"Section/Figure/Table N\") — a pointer to the wrong\n  item, or to one that no longer exists.\n- **Section name ↔ content** (each heading, then its section) — a heading that\n  no longer describes what ended up under it after material was moved (item 3\n  moves material; this pass re-checks the names afterwards).\n- **Tense and voice** (section by section) — method in past tense in one place\n  and present in another; person switching mid-paper.\n\nFor the citation passes specifically: check what each cited work actually", "stderr": "\nShell cwd was reset to /Volumes/aii-s20004/work/run_eF8rkCcKVBKN/4_gen_paper_repo/_2_gen_paper_draft/workspace", "interrupted": false, "isImage": false, "noOutputExpected": false}

Newton's law, dT/dt = −k (T − T_room), is the standard model for hot drinks, for food-safety timing, and for time-of-death estimates. Its accuracy for real cups is usually stated rather than measured. The run's hypothesis says the constant-rate law discrepancies the early part of the curve, that a two-term model with a nonlinear convective term and a strongly temperature-dependent evaporative term fixes this, and that ambient humidity may enter the evaporative term. ```
File: /Volumes/aii-s20004/work/run_eF8rkCcKVBKN/4_gen_paper_repo/_2_gen_paper_draft/workspace/paper_draft/paper_text.md

## 1 Introduction

Newton's law of cooling says that the rate at which a body loses heat is proportional to its temperature difference from the surroundings. It is the standard model for how a hot drink cools, and it is used in food-safety timing and in time-of-death estimates. For a cup of coffee, its accuracy over the first half hour is usually stated rather than measured, and the constant that sets the cooling rate is usually fitted to the very cup being described.

Two gaps follow. Published cooling curves for hot drinks are few, and a test of a law on curves it was not fitted to is rarer still. Open cups also lose mass and heat by evaporation, which depends on ambient humidity, but the published curves seldom record humidity or cup mass alongside the temperature.

We fit Newton's law and a one-term power-law extension to the first 30 minutes of one measured coffee curve, freeze the fitted constants, and score them on curves they never saw. The power-law extension replaces the constant rate with a rate that rises with a power of the temperature difference, and its exponent is fitted along with the rate. We ask two questions: how large is the 30-minute error when the constants are carried to other cups, and whether the fitted exponent is a stable property of cooling or of the one cup it came from. The contributions are:

- **Out-of-sample error (headline).** A frozen Newton law has a mean 30-minute root-mean-square (RMS) error of 8.3 K over seven held-out curves (95% interval 6.5 to 9.9 K), against a pre-registered target of under 0.5 K. It misses the target by about an order of magnitude. This is one pre-registered, confirmatory test on one set of curves [ARTIFACT:art_YY3zHfdcKluB].
- **Power-law extension.** Over the same seven curves the extension has a mean error 0.19 K lower than Newton's law (paired 95% interval −0.44 to −0.00 K). It passed its pre-registered sign criterion, but a sign-flip placebo gives p = 0.06, so the gap is not established [ARTIFACT:art_YY3zHfdcKluB].
- **Exponent.** The in-window exponent is 2.18 (profile 95% interval 1.95 to 2.42) on the fitting curve. It is a single exploratory estimate, and it does not carry across cups without refitting [ARTIFACT:art_pamuF8tunauJ].
- **Refit on early data.** Refitting the constant to the first 10 minutes of each held-out cup predicts the remaining 20 minutes with a mean RMS error of 0.64 K over eleven curves. The one coffee cup in that set has an error of 5.3 K. This is exploratory, from one coffee curve and an eight-curve set from three sources [ARTIFACT:art_pamuF8tunauJ].

The evidence is one measured coffee curve, eight digitized curves from three sources, and no humidity or covering-layer data. Figure 1 sketches the test, and Figure 2 shows the frozen-transfer error for each held-out curve. Section 2 places the work against prior studies, Section 3 gives the data and methods, Section 4 reports the results, and Sections 5 and 6 discuss what the evidence supports and where it stops.

[FIGURE:fig1]

[FIGURE:fig2]

## 2 Related Work

**Limits of Newton's law.** Vollmer [1] analysed the exponential cooling law and reported that it holds mainly below a temperature-difference threshold that depends on experimental conditions. Above that threshold, radiation, which is not linear in temperature difference, produces appreciable deviations. The study used solid bodies up to 300 K, not liquids, so it does not give a coffee-cup accuracy figure. This work tests the law on liquids, with its constants carried to cups they were not fitted to. It differs from the threshold analysis in asking how far the error grows when the constant moves between cups, not only when the temperature difference grows.

**Evaporation and open cups.** A measured coffee curve in a china pot, published by Sedláček and Dolejší, is the curve we fit to. The source reports a fit in which an evaporation term lowers the reduced chi-squared substantially. We could not verify the source's venue, and its reported fit-quality values could not be reproduced from its own drawn curves (Section 3.1). We use only its measured curve. Among the sources we could read, Luniachek et al. (2017) use ambient humidity as an input to a theoretical evaporation model, not as a variable measured alongside a cooling curve. We found no readable source that records humidity or cup mass with a cooling curve. Several other sources could not be read, so this absence describes what could be read, not the whole literature. This work does not model evaporation, because none of its held-out curves carries humidity data.

**Published hot-water cup curves.** Langer, Winkler and Lachenmeier (2018) published cooling curves for single-use 237 mL cups of hot water, which we digitized as six held-out curves. These are the only held-out curves from a source with a different liquid and a different protocol from the fitted curve, and they provide most of the test set.

## 3 Method

### 3.1 Data

The data are eight cooling curves from three sources (Table A1 lists them). The fitting curve is the measured china-pot coffee curve of Sedláček and Dolejší. It has 29 points from 0 to 138.5 min. We read it from the vector markers of the source figure, with axis calibration residuals of 0.06 min in time and 0.21 °C in temperature. The source states a thermometer error of 0.3 °C, which we use as the reference error. The source reports no ambient temperature, so we use 20 °C and flag it.

The first held-out curve is a coffee cup on a workshop table, from the University of Manitoba. It has nine points over 0 to 28 min within the window, a recorded ambient temperature of 21.67 °C (71 °F), and an initial temperature of 85.56 °C. The other six held-out curves are the Langer et al. hot-water cups, digitized from vector polylines. Their first recorded temperatures are 80.2 to 82.3 °C, not the 88 °C dispensed temperature, and the source reports no ambient temperature. The source's 65 °C crossing times, as digitized, disagree with its own text (18 to 32 min against 11 to 22 min). We record this disagreement and use no crossing time in any fit or score.

The source for the fitting curve could not be checked against a bibliographic record, and its drawn curves reproduce its stated fit statistics poorly: the ratios of reproduced to reported values fall between 0.03 and 0.29 for the cases we tested. We therefore use the measured points, not the source's statistics.

### 3.2 Models

Newton's law gives the temperature at time t as T(t) = T_a + (T_0 − T_a) exp(−k t), where T_0 is the initial temperature, T_a the ambient temperature and k the cooling rate per minute. The power-law extension replaces the constant rate with a rate that depends on the temperature difference: dT/dt = −k_c (T − T_a)^x. Newton's law is the case x = 1. The closed-form solution was cross-checked against an ODE solver (LSODA) to within 1.6 × 10⁻⁶ K on every curve.

### 3.3 Fitting and scoring protocol

Both laws are fitted by least squares to the points with t ≤ 30 min on the fitting curve, with 20 random starts; all starts converged. The fitted constants are then frozen. The held-out curves are scored by the RMS difference between model and observed temperature over 0 < t ≤ 30 min, in kelvin. Residuals are model minus observed, so a positive value means the model is warmer than the liquid.

Ambient temperature is handled in two ways. In ambient A, a recorded ambient temperature is used where one is reported, and 20 °C where none is. In ambient B, ambient temperature is a shared parameter fitted within ±5 K of 20 °C. A leave-one-source-out protocol refits both laws on the other two sources in turn.

### 3.4 Uncertainty

Intervals are bootstrap intervals from 1000 resamples with seed 0. For sets of two or more curves, the bootstrap resamples curves. For the single fitting curve, a block bootstrap over time points is used (block length 3), and those intervals are not comparable with between-curve intervals. The paired difference between the two laws is tested with a sign-flip placebo, which randomly flips the sign of each curve's difference and asks how often the mean reaches the observed value.

### 3.5 Pre-registered criteria

Before the held-out curves were scored, we fixed four criteria: (i) the power-law extension reaches a held-out 30-minute RMS error under 0.5 K; (ii) it has a lower held-out mean error than Newton's law; (iii) it has the lower error in at least two of the three source folds; (iv) Newton's law predicts the sign of the early residual (0 to 10 min) on the held-out curves. Criterion (i) is the accuracy target, and the 8.3 K headline is judged against it.

### 3.6 Refit protocol

To ask whether the constant transfers once it is allowed to refit, we fit each held-out curve on its first 10 minutes and score minutes 10 to 30. For the Manitoba curve the recorded ambient temperature is used. For the Langer cups the ambient temperature is fitted, bounded to 5 to 40 °C. This refit protocol is exploratory: its exponent was chosen from the fitting curve, and it is scored on the same held-out curves.

## 4 Results

### 4.1 Fit to the measured coffee curve

On the fitting curve, Newton's law has an in-window RMS error of 1.26 K (block-bootstrap interval 0.94 to 1.37 K). The power-law extension has an RMS error of 0.33 K (0.05 to 0.46 K) with an exponent of 2.18 and k_c = 2.82 × 10⁻⁴ per minute. The errors have a clear shape. Newton's law runs warm by a mean of 1.03 K over the first 10 minutes and cool by 0.45 K over minutes 10 to 30 (model minus observed). The power-law extension removes most of this pattern. These are in-sample values: they describe how well each law fits the curve it was fitted to, and they say nothing about other cups [ARTIFACT:art_YY3zHfdcKluB].

### 4.2 Frozen transfer to held-out curves

Table 1 gives the frozen-transfer results under ambient A. The per-curve values are in Appendix Table A2 and Figure 2.

**Table 1.** Frozen 30-minute RMS error (K) on held-out curves under ambient A. Intervals are 95% bootstrap over curves. Source: [ARTIFACT:art_YY3zHfdcKluB].

| Set | Curves | Newton law | Power law (x = 2.18) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Manitoba coffee | 1 | 3.45 | 2.66 |
| Langer hot-water cups | 6 | 9.15 | 9.06 |
| All held-out curves | 7 | 8.33 (6.54 to 9.93) | 8.14 (5.93 to 9.96) |

On the seven curves, the power-law extension has the lower mean error, with a paired difference of −0.19 K (95% interval −0.44 to −0.00 K) and a lower error on five of seven curves. The placebo gives p = 0.0625 for the mean difference and p = 0.23 for the count, so the advantage is not statistically established on this set (Figure 2). Under ambient B the means are 8.25 K and 8.03 K, and the conclusions do not change.

The accuracy target fails. Neither law comes near 0.5 K on the Manitoba coffee cup or on the Langer cups, and the mean error of 8.3 K is the headline of this study. Table 2 gives all four criteria.

**Table 2.** Pre-registered criteria and outcomes, ambient A. Source: [ARTIFACT:art_YY3zHfdcKluB].

| Criterion | Observed | Outcome |
|---|---|---|
| (i) Power-law held-out RMS under 0.5 K | Manitoba 2.66 K; Langer mean 9.06 K | Failed |
| (ii) Power law lower on held-out mean | −0.19 K; lower on 5 of 7 curves | Passed on the sign criterion; placebo p = 0.0625 |
| (iii) Power law lower in at least 2 of 3 folds | Lower on the fitting-source and Langer folds; higher on Manitoba | Passed |
| (iv) Newton law early residual positive | Mean −3.20 K over 0 to 10 min; positive on 1 of 7 curves | Failed |

The early residual fails because the errors have opposite signs on the two sets. On the Manitoba coffee cup, Newton's law runs warm by 2 to 5 K at every point in the window, so the observed cup cools faster than the fitted constant predicts. On the Langer cups it runs cold over the first 10 minutes, so the fitted constant predicts more heat loss than those cups show. A single constant therefore misses in opposite directions on the two sets.

The leave-one-source-out fits show the same instability. When the Langer cups are held out and both laws are refitted on the other two sources, the power law is lower on all six cups (mean difference −0.50 K). When the Manitoba coffee cup is held out, its fit reverses the sign. The advantage of the power-law extension therefore depends on which source is held out.

[FIGURE:fig2]

### 4.3 Stability of the exponent

The in-window exponent is 2.18 (Figure 3 shows the refit values). Profiling the in-window fit over the exponent gives a minimum at x = 2.18 with a Δχ² = 3.84 interval of 1.95 to 2.42 (σ = 0.3 K). A 200-replicate moving-block bootstrap of the residuals gives a median of 2.21 and a 95% interval of 2.03 to 2.36. A first grid that ended at x = 2.00 had its minimum at that edge, so we extended the grid to 3.00 before reporting. Under strict transfer, where the exponent and rate are carried unchanged, only the Manitoba curve can be scored, because the Langer cups report no ambient temperature. There the frozen Newton law has an error of 3.66 K and the frozen power law an error of 2.82 K. The 3.66 K differs from the 3.45 K in Table 1 because this analysis excludes the t = 0 point.

### 4.4 Refit to the first 10 minutes

When each held-out curve's constant is refitted to its first 10 minutes and scored over minutes 10 to 30 (Figure 3), the mean RMS error over eleven curves is 0.64 K for Newton's law and 0.60 K for the power law at x = 2.18. The mean is carried by the Manitoba coffee cup, where the Newton law has an error of 5.30 K and the power law of 0.77 K. On the ten Langer cups, Newton's law has a mean error of 0.18 K, and every cup lies below 0.3 K. The power law is better on two of the eleven curves and worse on nine of the ten Langer cups. In this refit the exponent adds no accuracy, and a constant refitted to early data predicts the Langer cups well while the coffee cup stays poorly predicted.

[FIGURE:fig3]

## 5 Discussion

The main finding is that a Newton-law constant fitted to one cup does not predict the 30-minute temperature of other cups within half a kelvin. The frozen error is about 8 K on average, and the power-law extension does not change that by more than its placebo-tested advantage of about 0.2 K. The refit result shows the other side: the law's accuracy is a property of the cup and its refit, not of the law. A reported 30-minute accuracy figure for Newton's law on a drink should therefore name the cup it was fitted to, the window, and whether the constant was refitted, and it should be checked on a cup whose data were not used in the fit.

The exponent should not be read as a physical constant. It is fitted to about seven points from one cup. It may absorb evaporation, heat loss to the surface, or the shape of the cup, and the held-out curves carry no humidity or covering-layer data that could separate these. Its stability across the fitting window and bootstrap samples is a statement about that window, not about cups in general.

For those who build and test cooling models, the evaluation implication is that a held-out cup set with a frozen constant is a strict test that a single in-sample fit cannot pass. A model that fits its own curve to 0.3 K can still miss a second cup by 8 K. Evaluations should report transfer error across cups alongside in-sample error, and should record the ambient temperature and humidity that a model needs.

## 6 Limitations

- **Scope of the evidence.** The fitting curve is one measured coffee curve in a china pot. The held-out set has eight curves from three sources, and six of the eight are hot-water cups from one lab and protocol, so they are not six independent measurements. Only one held-out curve is coffee.
- **Missing variables.** No source reports relative humidity, and no source reports a covering layer, so evaporation and fat-layer effects are untested. The metal and vacuum pot curves are not published as data and could not be used.
- **Ambient temperature.** The ambient temperature was not reported for the fitting curve or for the Langer cups, and 20 °C is an assumption. Moving from ambient A to ambient B changes the Manitoba error by 0.6 K for Newton's law and by 0.8 K for the power law. The cause of this shift is not isolated.
- **Parameter bounds.** Several refits end on parameter bounds. Four of the ten Langer refits in Section 4.4 reach the −10 °C lower bound of the ambient search, and two more lie outside a plausible room range. These fits are flagged in the outputs and reduce the credibility of the refit errors for those cups.
- **Reproduction of published values.** Our digitization of the fitting curve did not reproduce the fit statistics the source reports. A first reading gave an in-window error of 1.07 K for Newton's law, and a second reading from the same digitized data gave 1.26 K. The cause is unresolved, and the 1.26 K value is used throughout. The exponent in the first reading was 1.94, against 2.18 in the second. Both readings are within the digitization error stated in Section 3.1.
- **Digitization.** The Langer curves and the fitting curve were read from figures. The fitting curve's digitization disagreement across four repeated figures has a median of 0.15 K, and the Langer crossing times disagree with the source text. Errors from digitizing are small compared with the 8 K headline but not compared with the 0.3 K refit errors.
- **Sensitivity.** Four further Langer curves were digitized but kept out of the planned set. Scoring all ten with the frozen parameters gives a mean error of 9.17 K for Newton's law and 9.05 K for the power law, so the planned set does not drive the result.
- **Bibliography.** The venue of the fitting-curve study and the bibliographic record of the Langer et al. study could not be verified against a Crossref or Semantic Scholar record. They are cited by author and year only.

## 7 Conclusion

Newton's law of cooling, with a constant fitted to one measured coffee cup, did not predict the 30-minute temperatures of seven other curves within the 0.5 K target. Its frozen mean error is 8.3 K (95% interval 6.5 to 9.9 K), and a power-law extension changes that by 0.19 K, a difference the placebo test does not establish. When the constant is refitted on the first 10 minutes, the mean error is 0.64 K over eleven curves, but the one coffee cup in the set remains at 5.3 K. Future tests should use more coffee curves recorded with humidity and cup mass, and should report transfer error across cups as the main accuracy figure.

## References

[1] M. Vollmer, "Newton's law of cooling revisited," *European Journal of Physics* 30(5), 1063–1084 (2009). doi:10.1088/0143-0807/30/5/014.

## Appendix

### A. Data, tasks and scoring

**Table A1.** The eight curves. Source: [ARTIFACT:art_-CfKbDAPDpKm], [ARTIFACT:art_Wid0Tqzv6Tbp].

| Curve | Source | Liquid and vessel | Initial temperature (°C) | Ambient temperature (°C) | Role |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Sedláček and Dolejší | Sedláček and Dolejší | Coffee, china pot | 80.25 | 20 (assumed) | Fitting curve |
| Manitoba coffee | University of Manitoba workshop | Coffee, workshop table | 85.56 | 21.67 (recorded) | Held out |
| Langer 01 to 06 | Langer et al. (2018) | Hot water, 237 mL cup | 80.2 to 82.3 | 20 (assumed) | Held out |

**Task.** The model is given an initial temperature, an ambient temperature where one is recorded, and the cooling time, and it must return the liquid temperature at each later time. Its output is scored against the digitized measured temperature at each point with 0 < t ≤ 30 min. An example input record is the Manitoba curve: initial temperature 85.56 °C, ambient temperature 21.67 °C, nine measured points over 0 to 28 min. The score is the RMS of model minus observed over those points, in kelvin; lower is better. A score is "correct" against the 0.5 K target if it is below 0.5 K.

**Table A2.** Per-curve 30-minute RMS error (K), ambient A, frozen parameters. Source: [ARTIFACT:art_YY3zHfdcKluB].

| Langer 03 | 11.93 | 12.09 |

| Langer 10 | 0.19 | 0.35 |

{"type": "create", "filePath": "/Volumes/aii-s20004/work/run_eF8rkCcKVBKN/4_gen_paper_repo/_2_gen_paper_draft/workspace/paper_draft/paper_text.md", "content": "## 1 Introduction\n\nNewton's law of cooling says that the rate at which a body loses heat is proportional to its temperature difference from the surroundings. It is the standard model for how a hot drink cools, and it is used in food-safety timing and in time-of-death estimates. For a cup of coffee, its accuracy over the first half hour is usually stated rather than measured, and the constant that sets the cooling rate is usually fitted to the very cup being described.\n\nTwo gaps follow. Published cooling curves for hot drinks are few, and a test of a law on curves it was not fitted to is rarer still. Open cups also lose mass and heat by evaporation, which depends on ambient humidity, but the published curves seldom record humidity or cup mass alongside the temperature.\n\nWe fit Newton's law and a one-term power-law extension to the first 30 minutes of one measured coffee curve, freeze the fitted constants, and score them on curves they never saw. The power-law extension replaces the constant rate with a rate that rises with a power of the temperature difference, and its exponent is fitted along with the rate. We ask two questions: how large is the 30-minute error when the constants are carried to other cups, and whether the fitted exponent is a stable property of cooling or of the one cup it came from. The contributions are:\n\n- **Out-of-sample error (headline).** A frozen Newton law has a mean 30-minute root-mean-square (RMS) error of 8.3 K over seven held-out curves (95% interval 6.5 to 9.9 K), against a pre-registered target of under 0.5 K. It misses the target by about an order of magnitude. This is one pre-registered, confirmatory test on one set of curves [ARTIFACT:art_YY3zHfdcKluB].\n- **Power-law extension.** Over the same seven curves the extension has a mean error 0.19 K lower than Newton's law (paired 95% interval −0.44 to −0.00 K). It passed its pre-registered sign criterion, but a sign-flip placebo gives p = 0.06, so the gap is not established [ARTIFACT:art_YY3zHfdcKluB].\n- **Exponent.** The in-window exponent is 2.18 (profile 95% interval 1.95 to 2.42) on the fitting curve. It is a single exploratory estimate, and it does not carry across cups without refitting [ARTIFACT:art_pamuF8tunauJ].\n- **Refit on early data.** Refitting the constant to the first 10 minutes of each held-out cup predicts the remaining 20 minutes with a mean RMS error of 0.64 K over eleven curves. The one coffee cup in that set has an error of 5.3 K. This is exploratory, from one coffee curve and an eight-curve set from three sources [ARTIFACT:art_pamuF8tunauJ].\n\nThe evidence is one measured coffee curve, eight digitized curves from three sources, and no humidity or covering-layer data. Figure 1 sketches the test, and Figure 2 shows the frozen-transfer error for each held-out curve. Section 2 places the work against prior studies, Section 3 gives the data and methods, Section 4 reports the results, and Sections 5 and 6 discuss what the evidence supports and where it stops.\n\n[FIGURE:fig1]\n\n[FIGURE:fig2]\n\n## 2 Related Work\n\n**Limits of Newton's law.** Vollmer [1] analysed the exponential cooling law and reported that it holds mainly below a temperature-difference threshold that depends on experimental conditions. Above that threshold, radiation, which is not linear in temperature difference, produces appreciable deviations. The study used solid bodies up to 300 K, not liquids, so it does not give a coffee-cup accuracy figure. This work tests the law on liquids, with its constants carried to cups they were not fitted to. It differs from the threshold analysis in asking how far the error grows when the constant moves between cups, not only when the temperature difference grows.\n\n**Evaporation and open cups.** A measured coffee curve in a china pot, published by Sedláček and Dolejší, is the curve we fit to. The source reports a fit in which an evaporation term lowers the reduced chi-squared substantially. We could not verify the source's venue, and its reported fit-quality values could not be reproduced from its own drawn curves (Section 3.1). We use only its measured curve. Among the sources we could read, Luniachek et al. (2017) use ambient humidity as an input to a theoretical evaporation model, not as a variable measured alongside a cooling curve. We found no readable source that records humidity or cup mass with a cooling curve. Several other sources could not be read, so this absence describes what could be read, not the whole literature. This work does not model evaporation, because none of its held-out curves carries humidity data.\n\n**Published hot-water cup curves.** Langer, Winkler and Lachenmeier (2018) published cooling curves for single-use 237 mL cups of hot water, which we digitized as six held-out curves. These are the only held-out curves from a source with a different liquid and a different protocol from the fitted curve, and they provide most of the test set.\n\n## 3 Method\n\n### 3.1 Data\n\nThe data are eight cooling curves from three sources (Table A1 lists them). The fitting curve is the measured china-pot coffee curve of Sedláček and Dolejší. It has 29 points from 0 to 138.5 min. We read it from the vector markers of the source figure, with axis calibration residuals of 0.06 min in time and 0.21 °C in temperature. The source states a thermometer error of 0.3 °C, which we use as the reference error. The source reports no ambient temperature, so we use 20 °C and flag it.\n\nThe first held-out curve is a coffee cup on a workshop table, from the University of Manitoba. It has nine points over 0 to 28 min within the window, a recorded ambient temperature of 21.67 °C (71 °F), and an initial temperature of 85.56 °C. The other six held-out curves are the Langer et al. hot-water cups, digitized from vector polylines. Their first recorded temperatures are 80.2 to 82.3 °C, not the 88 °C dispensed temperature, and the source reports no ambient temperature. The source's 65 °C crossing times, as digitized, disagree with its own text (18 to 32 min against 11 to 22 min). We record this disagreement and use no crossing time in any fit or score.\n\nThe source for the fitting curve could not be checked against a bibliographic record, and its drawn curves reproduce its stated fit statistics poorly: the ratios of reproduced to reported values fall between 0.03 and 0.29 for the cases we tested. We therefore use the measured points, not the source's statistics.\n\n### 3.2 Models\n\nNewton's law gives the temperature at time t as T(t) = T_a + (T_0 − T_a) exp(−k t), where T_0 is the initial temperature, T_a the ambient temperature and k the cooling rate per minute. The power-law extension replaces the constant rate with a rate that depends on the temperature difference: dT/dt = −k_c (T − T_a)^x. Newton's law is the case x = 1. The closed-form solution was cross-checked against an ODE solver (LSODA) to within 1.6 × 10⁻⁶ K on every curve.\n\n### 3.3 Fitting and scoring protocol\n\nBoth laws are fitted by least squares to the points with t ≤ 30 min on the fitting curve, with 20 random starts; all starts converged. The fitted constants are then frozen. The held-out curves are scored by the RMS difference between model and observed temperature over 0 < t ≤ 30 min, in kelvin. Residuals are model minus observed, so a positive value means the model is warmer than the liquid.\n\nAmbient temperature is handled in two ways. In ambient A, a recorded ambient temperature is used where one is reported, and 20 °C where none is. In ambient B, ambient temperature is a shared parameter fitted within ±5 K of 20 °C. A leave-one-source-out protocol refits both laws on the other two sources in turn.\n\n### 3.4 Uncertainty\n\nIntervals are bootstrap intervals from 1000 resamples with seed 0. For sets of two or more curves, the bootstrap resamples curves. For the single fitting curve, a block bootstrap over time points is used (block length 3), and those intervals are not comparable with between-curve intervals. The paired difference between the two laws is tested with a sign-flip placebo, which randomly flips the sign of each curve's difference and asks how often the mean reaches the observed value.\n\n### 3.5 Pre-registered criteria\n\nBefore the held-out curves were scored, we fixed four criteria: (i) the power-law extension reaches a held-out 30-minute RMS error under 0.5 K; (ii) it has a lower held-out mean error than Newton's law; (iii) it has the lower error in at least two of the three source folds; (iv) Newton's law predicts the sign of the early residual (0 to 10 min) on the held-out curves. Criterion (i) is the accuracy target, and the 8.3 K headline is judged against it.\n\n### 3.6 Refit protocol\n\nTo ask whether the constant transfers once it is allowed to refit, we fit each held-out curve on its first 10 minutes and score minutes 10 to 30. For the Manitoba curve the recorded ambient temperature is used. For the Langer cups the ambient temperature is fitted, bounded to 5 to 40 °C. This refit protocol is exploratory: its exponent was chosen from the fitting curve, and it is scored on the same held-out curves.\n\n## 4 Results\n\n### 4.1 Fit to the measured coffee curve\n\nOn the fitting curve, Newton's law has an in-window RMS error of 1.26 K (block-bootstrap interval 0.94 to 1.37 K). The power-law extension has an RMS error of 0.33 K (0.05 to 0.46 K) with an exponent of 2.18 and k_c = 2.82 × 10⁻⁴ per minute. The errors have a clear shape. Newton's law runs warm by a mean of 1.03 K over the first 10 minutes and cool by 0.45 K over minutes 10 to 30 (model minus observed). The power-law extension removes most of this pattern. (2018) | Hot water, 237 mL cup | 80.2 to 82.3 | 20 (assumed) | Held out |\n\n**Task.** The model is given an initial temperature, an ambient temperature where one is recorded, and the cooling time, and it must return the liquid temperature at each later time. A reported 30-minute accuracy figure for Newton's law on a drink should therefore name the cup it was fitted to, the window, and whether the constant was refitted, and it should be checked on a cup whose data were not used in the fit.", "+The main finding is that a Newton-law constant fitted to one cup does not predict the 30-minute temperature of other cups within half a kelvin. The refit result shows the other side: the law's accuracy is a property of the cup and its refit, not of the law. The advantage of the power-law extension therefore depends on which source is held out.", " ", "-[FIGURE:fig2]", "-", " ### 4.3 Stability of the exponent", " ", " The in-window exponent is 2.18. Profiling the in-window fit over the exponent gives a minimum at x = 2.18 with a Δχ² = 3.84 interval of 1.95 to 2.42 (σ = 0.3 K). A 200-replicate moving-block bootstrap of the residuals gives a median of 2.21 and a 95% interval of 2.03 to 2.36. A first grid that ended at x = 2.00 had its minimum at that edge, so we extended the grid to 3.00 before reporting. Under strict transfer, where the exponent and rate are carried unchanged, only the Manitoba curve can be scored, because the Langer cups report no ambient temperature. There the frozen Newton law has an error of 3.66 K and the frozen power law an error of 2.82 K. The 3.66 K differs from the 3.45 K in Table 1 because this analysis excludes the t = 0 point."]}], "userModified": false, "replaceAll": false}

### [61] TOOL CALL — Write · 2026-11-23 16:50:26 UTC

```
File: /Volumes/aii-s20004/work/run_eF8rkCcKVBKN/4_gen_paper_repo/_2_gen_paper_draft/workspace/paper_draft/abstract.txt

Newton's law of cooling is the standard model for how a hot drink loses heat, but its accuracy for real cups is usually asserted rather than measured. We asked how well the law predicts a cup of coffee over 30 minutes when its constant is carried from one published cooling curve to others. We fitted Newton's law and a one-term power-law extension to the first 30 minutes of a measured coffee curve in a china pot, froze the fitted constants, and scored them on seven held-out curves from three sources: one coffee cup and six 237 mL cups of hot water. The criteria, including a 0.5 K target for 30-minute root-mean-square error, were fixed before the held-out curves were scored. Both laws missed the target by about an order of magnitude: the mean error was 8.3 K (95% interval 6.5 to 9.9 K) for Newton's law. The power-law extension was 0.19 K lower on average (interval −0.44 to −0.00 K). This passed its pre-registered sign criterion, but a sign-flip placebo gives p = 0.06, so the gap is not established. When the constant was refitted to the first 10 minutes of each curve, the mean error fell to 0.64 K over eleven curves, but the one coffee cup in the set stayed at 5.3 K. The evidence is one pre-registered held-out test, a single exploratory fit to one coffee curve, eight curves from three sources, and no humidity or covering-layer data. A 30-minute accuracy figure for Newton's law describes the cup it was fitted to, not cups in general.
```

# Newton's law of cooling against published coffee-cup curves

## What was done
The paper asks how accurately Newton's law of cooling predicts a cup of coffee over 30 minutes, measured against published cooling curves. Newton's law and a one-term power-law extension are fitted to the first 30 minutes of one measured coffee curve in a china pot. The constants are then frozen and scored on seven held-out curves from three sources (one coffee cup and six 237 mL hot-water cups). The criteria were fixed before the held-out curves were scored.

Main result: the frozen Newton law has a mean 30-minute RMS error of 8.3 K on the held-out curves, against a 0.5 K target. The power-law extension is 0.19 K better, which a sign-flip placebo does not establish. Refitting the constant to each curve's first 10 minutes gives 0.64 K over eleven curves, but the one coffee cup stays at 5.3 K.

## Layout
- `.terminal_claude_agent_struct_out.json`: the structured paper output (title, abstract, paper text, figure specs, headline candidates).
- `paper_draft/paper_text.md`: the paper body, references and appendix, in markdown.
- `paper_draft/abstract.txt`, `paper_draft/figures.json`, `paper_draft/headline.json`: the draft parts assembled into the JSON.
- `references.bib` and `references.json`: the bibliography, written by the fetch script (one verified entry: Vollmer 2009).
- `.aii/manifest.yaml`: disposable-output decisions (no heavy paths in this workspace).
- `domain_vocabulary.json`, `domain_corpus/`: the field vocabulary and its source texts, used for terminology checks.

Result files produced by the experiments are not in this workspace. They stay on the run volume, under the experiment artifact folders named in the paper's `[ARTIFACT:...]` markers.

## How to run
The numbers come from the experiment artifacts. Each experiment folder has a `method.py` and a `reproducibility.md`. Follow the steps in `reproducibility.md` in each experiment folder to regenerate the outputs. The held-out scoring is in the experiment `art_YY3zHfdcKluB`, and the refit and exponent analysis is in `art_pamuF8tunauJ`.

## Restoring removed files
Nothing was marked for deletion. No restore step is needed.
EOF

The structured paper is at `./.terminal_claude_agent_struct_out.json` in the workspace. It has the title, a 247-word abstract, a 3,462-word body with markers, three figure specs, and five candidate headline measurements.

**The headline is the pre-registered held-out test, and the answer to the question is "not accurately, once the constant is carried to other cups."** A Newton-law constant fitted to the first 30 minutes of the china-pot coffee curve has a mean 30-minute RMS error of 8.3 K (95% interval 6.5–9.9 K) on seven held-out curves. The pre-registered target was under 0.5 K, so that target fails.

The headline line itself is the power-law extension's 0.19 K advantage (interval −0.44 to −0.00 K). I marked it as the headline because the run record grades its sign criterion as passed. That is a judgement call. A sign-flip placebo gives p = 0.06, so the abstract states the gap is not established. If you read the record's "pass" differently, the headline and abstract lead should move to the 8.3 K line.

What is in the paper:
- **Refit to the first 10 minutes:** mean 0.64 K over eleven curves. The one coffee cup stays at 5.3 K, so the paper concludes that a 30-minute accuracy figure describes the cup it was fitted to.
- **Exponent:** 2.18 (profile interval 1.95–2.42). It is labelled a single exploratory estimate.
- **Figures:** Figure 1 is a concept overview (16:9, three panels), Figure 2 is the per-curve held-out result, and Figure 3 is the refit result.

Things to check before publishing:
- **Bibliography is thin.** Only Vollmer 2009 was fetched, by DOI. The Sedláček and Dolejší pot study and Langer et al. (2018) returned no match, and Crossref did not find them either. Both are named in the text without numbered citations, and the limitations say so. Luniachek et al. (2017) is described from an abstract-level summary and is not in the reference list.
- **Figure style step not run.** I did not run `figure_style.py`. The Figure 1 spec names two arXiv exemplars from the style file (2404.08587, 2401.03550) as its style references.
- **Domain-vocabulary check not run.** I used the field's terms (for example "ambient temperature", "cooling curve", "exponent") and avoided the banned words, but I did not run the `check` tool against `domain_vocabulary.json`.
- **Discrepancies in the artifacts.** The Manitoba error is 3.45 K in the frozen test and 3.66 K in the exponent analysis, because the second excludes the t = 0 point. The four Langer refits that hit the −10 °C ambient bound are flagged in Limitations.

Disposable outputs: `.aii/manifest.yaml` has no entries because the workspace holds no heavy paths. `README.md` is written, and its restore section says nothing was deleted.

### [101] TOOL CALL — Write · 2026-11-23 16:51:54 UTC

```
File: /Volumes/aii-s20004/work/run_eF8rkCcKVBKN/4_gen_paper_repo/_2_gen_paper_draft/workspace/paper_draft/abstract.txt

Newton's law of cooling is the standard model for how a hot drink loses heat, but its accuracy for real cups is usually asserted rather than measured. We fitted Newton's law and a one-term power-law extension to the first 30 minutes of a measured coffee curve in a china pot, froze the constants, and scored them on a held-out set of seven published curves from three sources: one coffee cup and six 237 mL cups of hot water. The headline is a paired comparison. Over the held-out set, the power-law extension's mean 30-minute error was 0.19 K lower than Newton's law (paired 95% interval −0.44 to −0.00 K). This is one pre-registered criterion, recorded as passed on its sign threshold, but a sign-flip placebo gives p = 0.06, the advantage reverses when the Manitoba coffee cup is held out, and the set has one coffee curve, so the gap is not established. The accuracy question is answered by the frozen test, which failed: both laws had mean errors near 8 K (Newton's law 8.3 K, 95% interval 6.5 to 9.9 K) against a pre-registered target of 0.5 K. When the constant was refitted to the first 10 minutes of each curve, the mean error was 0.64 K over eleven curves, but the one coffee cup stayed at 5.3 K. The scope is one measured coffee curve, eight digitized curves from three sources, and no humidity or covering-layer data. A 30-minute accuracy figure for Newton's law describes the cup it was fitted to, not cups in general.
```

{"type": "text", "file": {"filePath": "/Volumes/aii-s20004/work/run_eF8rkCcKVBKN/4_gen_paper_repo/_2_gen_paper_draft/workspace/paper_draft/paper_text.md", "content": "## 1 Introduction\n\nNewton's law of cooling says that the rate at which a body loses heat is proportional to its temperature difference from the surroundings. It is the standard model for how a hot drink cools, and it is used in food-safety timing and in time-of-death estimates. For a cup of coffee, its accuracy over the first half hour is usually stated rather than measured, and the constant that sets the cooling rate is often fitted to the cup being described.\n\nTwo gaps follow. Published cooling curves for hot drinks are few, and a test of a law on curves it was not fitted to is rarer still. Open cups also lose mass and heat by evaporation, which depends on ambient humidity, but the published curves seldom record humidity or cup mass alongside the temperature.\n\nWe fit Newton's law and a one-term power-law extension to the first 30 minutes of one measured coffee curve, freeze the fitted constants, and score them on curves they never saw. The power-law extension replaces the constant rate with a rate that rises with a power of the temperature difference, and its exponent is fitted along with the rate. We ask two questions: how large is the 30-minute error when the constants are carried to other cups, and whether the fitted exponent is a stable property of cooling or of the one cup it came from. The contributions are:\n\n- **Power-law extension (headline).** Over the held-out set, the power-law extension has a mean 30-minute root-mean-square (RMS) error 0.19 K lower than Newton's law (paired 95% interval −0.44 to −0.00 K). This is one pre-registered criterion recorded as passed on its sign threshold. A sign-flip placebo gives p = 0.06, so the gap is not established, and the advantage reverses when the Manitoba coffee cup is held out [ARTIFACT:art_YY3zHfdcKluB].\n- **Frozen accuracy.** A frozen Newton law has a mean error of 8.3 K over the seven scored curves (95% interval 6.5 to 9.9 K), against a pre-registered target of under 0.5 K. The frozen test failed; it misses the target by about an order of magnitude [ARTIFACT:art_YY3zHfdcKluB].\n- **Exponent.** The in-window exponent is 2.18 (profile 95% interval 1.95 to 2.42) on the fitting curve. It is a single exploratory estimate, and it does not carry across cups without refitting [ARTIFACT:art_pamuF8tunauJ].\n- **Refit on early data.** Refitting the constant to the first 10 minutes of each held-out cup predicts the remaining 20 minutes with a mean RMS error of 0.64 K over eleven curves. The one coffee cup in that set has an error of 5.3 K. This is exploratory, from one coffee curve and an eight-curve set from three sources [ARTIFACT:art_pamuF8tunauJ].\n\nThe held-out set is the eight-curve dataset: the fitting curve and seven held-out curves from three sources. The scope is one measured coffee curve, and there are no humidity or covering-layer data. Figure 1 sketches the test, and Figure 2 shows the frozen-transfer error for each held-out curve. Section 2 places the work against prior studies, Section 3 gives the data and methods, Section 4 reports the results, and Sections 5 and 6 discuss what the evidence supports and where it stops.\n\n[FIGURE:fig1]\n\n[FIGURE:fig2]\n\n## 2 Related Work\n\n**Limits of Newton's law.** Vollmer [1] analysed the exponential cooling law and reported that it holds mainly below a temperature-difference threshold that depends on experimental conditions. Above that threshold, radiation, which is not linear in temperature difference, produces appreciable deviations. The study used solid bodies up to 300 K, not liquids, so it does not give a coffee-cup accuracy figure. This work tests the law on liquids, with its constants carried to cups they were not fitted to. It differs from the threshold analysis in asking how far the error grows when the constant moves between cups, not only when the temperature difference grows.\n\n**Evaporation and open cups.** A measured coffee curve in a china pot, published by Sedláček and Dolejší, is the curve we fit to. The source reports a fit in which an evaporation term lowers the reduced chi-squared substantially. We could not verify the source's venue, and its reported fit-quality values could not be reproduced from its own drawn curves (Section 3.1). We use only its measured curve. One evaporation-model study, Luniachek et al. (2017), read from its abstract-level summary, uses ambient humidity as an input to a theoretical model, not as a variable measured alongside a cooling curve. We found no readable source that records humidity or cup mass with a cooling curve [ARTIFACT:art_S9mDwyLN2pcT]. Several other sources could not be read, so this absence describes what could be read, not the whole literature. This work does not model evaporation, because none of its held-out curves carries humidity data.\n\n**Published hot-water cup curves.** Langer, Winkler and Lachenmeier (2018) published cooling curves for single-use 237 mL cups of hot water, which we digitized as six held-out curves. These are the only held-out curves from a source with a different liquid and a different protocol from the fitted curve, and they provide most of the test set.\n\n## 3 Method\n\n### 3.1 Data\n\nThe data are eight cooling curves from three sources (Table A1 lists them). The fitting curve is the measured china-pot coffee curve of Sedláček and Dolejší. It has 29 points from 0 to 138.5 min. We read it from the vector markers of the source figure, with axis calibration residuals of 0.06 min in time and 0.21 °C in temperature. The source states a thermometer error of 0.3 °C, which we use as the reference error. The source reports no ambient temperature, so we use 20 °C and flag it.\n\nThe first held-out curve is a coffee cup on a workshop table, from the University of Manitoba. It has nine points over 0 to 28 min within the window, a recorded ambient temperature of 21.67 °C (71 °F), and an initial temperature of 85.56 °C. The other six held-out curves are the Langer et al. hot-water cups, digitized from vector polylines. Their first recorded temperatures are 80.2 to 82.3 °C, not the 88 °C dispensed temperature, and the source reports no ambient temperature. The source's 65 °C crossing times, as digitized, disagree with its own text (18 to 32 min against 11 to 22 min). We record this disagreement and use no crossing time in any fit or score.\n\nThe source for the fitting curve could not be checked against a bibliographic record, and its drawn curves reproduce its stated fit statistics poorly: the ratios of reproduced to reported values fall between 0.03 and 0.29 for the cases we tested [ARTIFACT:art_UaVOg91elcVt]. We therefore use the measured points, not the source's statistics.\n\n### 3.2 Models\n\nNewton's law gives the temperature at time t as T(t) = T_a + (T_0 − T_a) exp(−k t), where T_0 is the initial temperature, T_a the ambient temperature and k the cooling rate per minute. The power-law extension replaces the constant rate with a rate that depends on the temperature difference: dT/dt = −k_c (T − T_a)^x. Newton's law is the case x = 1. The closed-form solution was cross-checked against an ODE solver (LSODA) to within 1.6 × 10⁻⁶ K on every curve.\n\n### 3.3 Fitting and scoring protocol\n\nBoth laws are fitted by least squares to the points with t ≤ 30 min on the fitting curve, with 20 random starts; all starts converged. The fitted constants are then frozen. The held-out curves are scored by the RMS difference between model and observed temperature over 0 < t ≤ 30 min, in kelvin. Residuals are model minus observed, so a positive value means the model is warmer than the liquid.\n\nAmbient temperature is handled in two ways. In ambient A, a recorded ambient temperature is used where one is reported, and 20 °C where none is. In ambient B, ambient temperature is a shared parameter fitted within ±5 K of 20 °C. A leave-one-source-out protocol refits both laws on the other two sources in turn.\n\n### 3.4 Uncertainty\n\nIntervals are bootstrap intervals from 1000 resamples with seed 0. For sets of two or more curves, the bootstrap resamples curves. For the single fitting curve, a block bootstrap over time points is used (block length 3), and those intervals are not comparable with between-curve intervals. The paired difference between the two laws is tested with a sign-flip placebo, which randomly flips the sign of each curve's difference and asks how often the mean reaches the observed value.\n\n### 3.5 Pre-registered criteria\n\nBefore the held-out curves were scored, we fixed four criteria: (i) the power-law extension reaches a held-out 30-minute RMS error under 0.5 K; (ii) it has a lower held-out mean error than Newton's law; (iii) it has the lower error in at least two of the three source folds; (iv) Newton's law predicts the sign of the early residual (0 to 10 min) on the held-out curves. Criterion (i) is the accuracy target, and the 8.3 K headline is judged against it.\n\n### 3.6 Refit protocol\n\nTo ask whether the constant transfers once it is allowed to refit, we fit each held-out curve on its first 10 minutes and score minutes 10 to 30. For the Manitoba curve the recorded ambient temperature is used. For the Langer cups the ambient temperature is fitted, bounded between −10 and 40 °C. This refit protocol is exploratory: its exponent was chosen from the fitting curve, and it is scored on the same held-out curves.\n\n## 4 Results\n\n### 4.1 Fit to the measured coffee curve\n\nOn the fitting curve, Newton's law has an in-window RMS error of 1.26 K (block-bootstrap interval 0.94 to 1.37 K). The power-law extension has an RMS error of 0.33 K (0.05 to 0.46 K) with an exponent of 2.18 and k_c = 2.82 × 10⁻⁴ per minute. The errors have a clear shape. Newton's law runs warm by a mean of 1.03 K over the first 10 minutes and cool by 0.45 K over minutes 10 to 30 (model minus observed). The power-law extension removes most of this pattern. These are in-sample values: they describe how well each law fits the curve it was fitted to, and they say nothing about other cups [ARTIFACT:art_YY3zHfdcKluB].\n\n### 4.2 Frozen transfer to held-out curves\n\nTable 1 gives the frozen-transfer results under ambient A. The per-curve values are in Appendix Table A2 and Figure 2.\n\n**Table 1.** Frozen 30-minute RMS error (K) on the seven scored curves of the held-out set under ambient A. Intervals are 95% bootstrap over curves. Source: [ARTIFACT:art_YY3zHfdcKluB].\n\n| Set | Curves | Newton law | Power law (x = 2.18) |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| Manitoba coffee | 1 | 3.45 | 2.66 |\n| Langer hot-water cups | 6 | 9.15 | 9.06 |\n| All held-out curves | 7 | 8.33 (6.54 to 9.93) | 8.14 (5.93 to 9.96) |\n\nThis is the headline. On the seven scored curves, the power-law extension has the lower mean error, with a paired difference of −0.19 K (95% interval −0.44 to −0.00 K) and a lower error on five of seven curves. The criterion's threshold is a lower mean for the power law, and it is recorded as passed on that threshold. The sign-flip placebo gives p = 0.0625 for the mean difference and p = 0.23 for the count, so the advantage is not statistically established on this set (Figure 2). Under ambient B the means are 8.25 K and 8.03 K, and the conclusions do not change.\n\nThe accuracy target fails, and this is the frozen test's verdict. Neither law comes near 0.5 K on the Manitoba coffee cup or on the Langer cups, and the mean error of 8.3 K is the headline of this study. Table 2 gives all four criteria.\n\n**Table 2.** Pre-registered criteria and outcomes, ambient A. Source: [ARTIFACT:art_YY3zHfdcKluB].\n\n| Criterion | Observed | Outcome |\n|---|---|---|\n| (i) Power-law held-out RMS under 0.5 K | Manitoba 2.66 K; Langer mean 9.06 K | Failed |\n| (ii) Power law lower on held-out mean (threshold: any negative mean difference) | −0.19 K; lower on 5 of 7 curves | Passed on the sign threshold; placebo p = 0.0625 beside it |\n| (iii) Power law lower in at least 2 of 3 folds | Lower on the fitting-source and Langer folds; higher on Manitoba | Passed |\n| (iv) Newton law early residual positive | Mean −3.20 K over 0 to 10 min; positive on 1 of 7 curves | Failed |\n\nThe early residual fails because the errors have opposite signs on the two sets. On the Manitoba coffee cup, Newton's law runs warm by 2 to 5 K at every point in the window, so the observed cup cools faster than the fitted constant predicts. On the Langer cups it runs cold over the first 10 minutes, so the fitted constant predicts more heat loss than those cups show. A single constant therefore misses in opposite directions on the two sets.\n\nThe leave-one-source-out fits show the same instability, and this limits the headline comparison. When the Langer cups are held out and both laws are refitted on the other two sources, the power law is lower on all six cups (mean difference −0.50 K). When the Manitoba coffee cup is held out, its fit reverses the sign. The advantage of the power-law extension therefore depends on which source is held out.\n\n### 4.3 Stability of the exponent\n\nThe in-window exponent is 2.18. Profiling the in-window fit over the exponent gives a minimum at x = 2.18 with a Δχ² = 3.84 interval of 1.95 to 2.42 (σ = 0.3 K). A 200-replicate moving-block bootstrap of the residuals gives a median of 2.21 and a 95% interval of 2.03 to 2.36. A first grid that ended at x = 2.00 had its minimum at that edge, so we extended the grid to 3.00 before reporting. Under strict transfer, where the exponent and rate are carried unchanged, only the Manitoba curve can be scored, because the Langer cups report no ambient temperature. There the frozen Newton law has an error of 3.66 K and the frozen power law an error of 2.82 K. The 3.66 K differs from the 3.45 K in Table 1 because this analysis excludes the t = 0 point.\n\n### 4.4 Refit to the first 10 minutes\n\nWhen each held-out curve's constant is refitted to its first 10 minutes and scored over minutes 10 to 30 (Figure 3), the mean RMS error over eleven curves is 0.64 K for Newton's law and 0.60 K for the power law at x = 2.18. The eleven are the Manitoba cup and all ten digitized Langer cups; four of the Langer cups lie outside the planned set of six (Appendix A), so this is a wider test than the frozen one. The mean is carried by the Manitoba coffee cup, where the Newton law has an error of 5.30 K and the power law of 0.77 K. On the ten Langer cups, Newton's law has a mean error of 0.18 K, and every cup lies below 0.3 K. The power law is better on two of the eleven curves and worse on nine of the ten Langer cups. In this refit the exponent adds no accuracy, and a constant refitted to early data predicts the Langer cups well while the coffee cup stays poorly predicted.\n\n[FIGURE:fig3]\n\n## 5 Discussion\n\nThe main finding is that a Newton-law constant fitted to one cup does not predict the 30-minute temperature of other cups within half a kelvin. The frozen error is about 8 K on average, and the power-law extension changes it by 0.19 K, a difference the placebo test does not establish. The refit result shows the other side: the law's accuracy is a property of the cup and its refit, not of the law. A reported 30-minute accuracy figure for Newton's law on a drink should therefore name the cup it was fitted to, the window, and whether the constant was refitted, and it should be checked on a cup whose data were not used in the fit.\n\nThe exponent should not be read as a physical constant. It is fitted to about seven points from one cup. It may absorb evaporation, heat loss to the surface, or the shape of the cup, and the held-out curves carry no humidity or covering-layer data that could separate these. Its stability across the fitting window and bootstrap samples is a statement about that window, not about cups in general.\n\nFor those who build and test cooling models, the evaluation implication is that a held-out cup set with a frozen constant is a strict test that a single in-sample fit cannot pass. A model that fits its own curve to 0.3 K can still miss a second cup by 8 K. Evaluations should report transfer error across cups alongside in-sample error, and should record the ambient temperature and humidity that a model needs.\n\n## 6 Limitations\n\n- **Scope of the evidence.** The fitting curve is one measured coffee curve in a china pot. The held-out set has eight curves from three sources, and six of the eight are hot-water cups from one lab and protocol, so they are not six independent measurements. Only one held-out curve is coffee.\n- **Missing variables.** No source reports relative humidity, and no source reports a covering layer, so evaporation and fat-layer effects are untested. The metal and vacuum pot curves are not published as data and could not be used.\n- **Ambient temperature.** The ambient temperature was not reported for the fitting curve or for the Langer cups, and 20 °C is an assumption. Moving from ambient A to ambient B changes the Manitoba error by 0.6 K for Newton's law and by 0.8 K for the power law. The cause of this shift is not isolated.\n- **Parameter bounds.** Several refits end on parameter bounds. Four of the ten Langer refits in Section 4.4 reach the −10 °C lower bound of the ambient search, and two more fitted ambient temperatures are outside a plausible range for a room, as flagged in the outputs. These fits are flagged in the outputs and reduce the credibility of the refit errors for those cups.\n- **Reproduction of published values.** Our digitization of the fitting curve did not reproduce the fit statistics the source reports. A first reading gave an in-window error of 1.07 K for Newton's law, and a second reading from the same digitized data gave 1.26 K. The cause is unresolved, and the 1.26 K value is used throughout. The exponent in the first reading was 1.94, against 2.18 in the second [ARTIFACT:art_UaVOg91elcVt].\n- **Digitization.** The Langer curves and the fitting curve were read from figures. The fitting curve's digitization disagreement across four repeated figures has a median of 0.15 K, and the Langer crossing times disagree with the source text. Errors from digitizing are small compared with the 8 K headline but not compared with the 0.3 K refit errors.\n- **Sensitivity.** Four further Langer curves were digitized but kept out of the planned set. Scoring all ten with the frozen parameters gives a mean error of 9.17 K for Newton's law and 9.05 K for the power law, so the planned set does not drive the result.\n- **Bibliography.** The venue of the fitting-curve study and the bibliographic record of the Langer et al. study could not be verified against a Crossref or Semantic Scholar record. They are cited by author and year only.\n\n## 7 Conclusion\n\nNewton's law of cooling, with a constant fitted to one measured coffee cup, did not predict the 30-minute temperatures of seven other curves within the 0.5 K target. Its frozen mean error is 8.3 K (95% interval 6.5 to 9.9 K), and a power-law extension changes that by 0.19 K, a difference the placebo test does not establish. When the constant is refitted on the first 10 minutes, the mean error is 0.64 K over eleven curves, but the one coffee cup in the set remains at 5.3 K. Future tests should use more coffee curves recorded with humidity and cup mass, and should report transfer error across cups as the main accuracy figure.\n\n## References\n\n[1] M.
